L54: ## 1 Executive Summary
L55: ## 1.1 Product Introduction
L56: Vimseltinib is a new molecular entity (NME), pursuant to regulations under 21 CFR 601. Vimseltinib (ROMVIMZA®) is an oral, small molecule kinase inhibitor of colony-stimulating factor 1 receptor (CSF1R). At the time of the New Drug Application (NDA) submission on June 17, 2024, vimseltinib was not approved for any indication in the United States or elsewhere.
L57: The Applicant's proposed indication at the time of the NDA submission was: ROMVIMZA is a kinase inhibitor indicated for treatment of adult patients with symptomatic tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT).
L58: ## 1.2 Conclusion on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L59: The review team concluded that the submitted data provide substantial evidence of efficacy with acceptable safety of vimseltinib for the treatment of adult patients with symptomatic TGCT for which surgical resection will potentially cause worsening functional limitation or severe morbidity. This conclusion is based on the results of Part 1 of Study DCC-3014-03-001 (MOTION), a double-blind, multicenter, randomized (2:1), placebo-controlled study in 123 adult patients with symptomatic TGCT for which surgical resection would potentially cause worsening functional limitation or severe morbidity, as judged by surgical consultation or a multidisciplinary tumor board. Symptomatic disease was defined as experiencing at least moderate pain or moderate stiffness within the screening period. Pain was assessed using the Brief Pain Inventory (BPI) Worst Pain numeric rating scale (NRS); stiffness was assessed using the Worst Stiffness NRS. A score of 4 or more out of 10 (with 10 being the worst condition) on either scale was required to define moderate pain or stiffness for inclusion purposes. Patients received vimseltinib (n=83) 30 mg twice weekly or placebo (n=40) through Week 24 during the double-blind (Part 1) part of the study. During the open-label period (Part 2), patients could continue to receive vimseltinib or crossover from placebo to receive vimseltinib until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. The primary efficacy endpoint was objective response rate (ORR) at Week 25 as assessed by a blinded independent radiological review (IRC) according to Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST) v1.1.
L60: The ORR in the primary efficacy analysis population of MOTION for the vimseltinib arm was 40% (95% confidence interval [CI]: 29, 51) with 4 (5%) patients experiencing a complete response. Of the 33 patients who experienced an objective response who were followed for an additional 6 months from the time of ORR primary analysis, duration of response (DOR) was \ge 6 months for 85% of patients and \ge 9 months for 58% of patients. There were no responders in the placebo arm.
L61: The key secondary endpoints and patient-reported outcome (PRO) endpoints included:
L62: - ORR per tumor volume score (TVS) at Week 25
- change from baseline in active range of motion (ROM) of the affected joint, relative to a reference standard, at Week 25
- change from baseline in the Patient-Reported Outcomes Measurement Information System-Physical Function (PROMIS-PF) score at Week 25
- change from baseline in the Worst Stiffness NRS score at Week 25
- change from baseline in EuroQoL Visual Analogue Scale (EQ-VAS) at Week 25, and
- response of at least a 30% improvement in the mean BPI Worst Pain NRS score without a 30% or greater increase in narcotic analgesic use at Week 25.
L63: In general, these secondary endpoints supported the primary endpoint results, and the review team included results of the change from baseline in ROM, PROMIS-PF, and BPI analyses in product labeling. Further discussion of these endpoint results are described in the review Sections 8.1.1. and 8.1.2
L64: TGCT is a rare, serious condition that is associated with significant morbidity; patients with TGCT represent a population with a high unmet medical need. The Applicant has demonstrated substantial evidence of effectiveness of vimseltinib in patients with TGCT based on clinically meaningful durable tumor shrinkage accompanied by supportive improvements in clinical outcome assessments including ROM, physical function, and pain. Therefore, the review team recommends granting traditional approval to vimseltinib for the treatment of adult patients with symptomatic TGCT for which surgical resection will potentially cause worsening functional limitation or severe morbidity. The review team modified the indication statement from the Applicant's proposed indication of "treatment of adult patients with symptomatic tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT)". In addition to requiring patients to have symptomatic disease as described previously, an additional inclusion criterion for patients enrolling in MOTION was that surgical resection would potentially cause worsening functional limitation or severe morbidity. The review team regarded this additional criterion to be essential as part of the indicated patient population given that local treatment with surgery can be effective if feasible, and patients for whom surgery was likely to result in good functional outcomes were not studied in MOTION. As such the review team revised the indication statement accordingly.
L65: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L66: ## 1.2. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L67: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L68: Tenosynovial giant cell tumors (TGCTs) are a rare, locally aggressive, non-malignant tumor that typically arises from the joints, bursa and tendon sheaths. TGCT is not life-threatening and does not metastasize but may cause significant morbidity impacting a patient's quality of life (Stacchiotti 2023). It exhibits a female predilection (2:1) and primarily affects patients between 20 and 50 years, although it can occur at any age. Because of the rarity of TGCT, there is very limited data in the literature regarding predilection for race or ethnicity. One study conducted in 83 patients with TGCT who received pexidartinib showed that 65% of patients were White, 18% per Other. 8% were Black, and 1.2% were missing. There were no Native Americans or Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander reported (Lin 2024). There are two subtypes of TGCT, diffuse and localized. Diffuse TGCT (dTGCT) tends to affect the larger joints, most commonly the knee and tends to be locally aggressive and have a higher recurrence rate of approximately 55%. Localized (ITGCT) disease commonly affects the digits and the wrists and has a recurrence rate of 15% (Ehrenstein 2017). The estimated overall annual incidence in the United States (US) of 11 cases per million; 1.8 cases per million for diffuse-type TGCT and 9.2 cases per million for localized TGCT (Lopez-Bastida 2021). From the largest TCGT registry worldwide, the Danish study reported the prevalence of ITGCT and dTGCT as 44/100,000 and 11/100,000, respectively. The 5-year recurrence-free survival (RFS) for ITGCT is 70–90 % and 30–80 % for dTGCT (Stacchiotti 2023).
L69: Surgery remains the mainstay of treatment for TGCT; however, there is no consensus on the optimal treatment as surgery can lead to joint damage, functional limitations, and recurrence (Stacchiotti 2023). In TGCT, a translocation in CSF1-COL6A3, t(1;2)(p13;q35-37), leads to overexpression of colony stimulating factor 1 (CSF1). In patients with unresectable relapsed and/or recurrent TGCT, agents that inhibit CSF1R, represent a logical therapeutic approach (Tap 2015). Pexidartinib was approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for patients with TGCT associated with severe morbidity or functional limitations and not amenable to surgery. However, in the pivotal study to support approval, ENLIVEN, 3 of 61 (5%) patients who received pexidartinib developed signs of serious liver injury. Hepatotoxicity including liver failure and life-threatening vanishing bile duct syndrome, ductopenia, and symptomatic cholestasis can occur. Thus, pexidartinib has a boxed warning for hepatotoxicity and a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) with Elements to Assure Safe Use (ETASU) to mitigate the risk of serious and potentially fatal liver injury. Per the National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN) guidelines for Soft Tissue Sarcoma v1.2023, other systemic treatment options include nilotinib and imatinib in certain circumstances. Radiation therapy may be in used in some certain circumstances including patients with relapsed disease or when surgery results in residual tumor. It has been reported to potentially reduce the risk of recurrence (Griffin 2012 and Stacchiotti 2023).
L70: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L71: The data submitted by the Applicant to support the efficacy of vimseltinib in patients with TGCT come from the MOTION study, an international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in 123 adult patients with symptomatic TGCT and forms the primary basis on which the Applicant is seeking approval of vimseltinib in the proposed indication. The major efficacy endpoint was ORR assessed by blinded IRC via RECIST v1.1. ORR at Week 25 in patients who received vimseltinib compared to placebo was 40% (95% CI: 29, 51) with 4 (5%) experiencing a complete response for patients on the vimseltinib arm and 0% (95% CI: 0, 9) for patients on the placebo arm (p-value &lt;0.0001). Of the 33 patients who experienced an objective response who were followed for an additional 6 months from the time of ORR primary analysis, duration of response was \ge6 months for 85% of patients and \ge9 months for 58% of patients. To support an overall favorable risk:benefit based on the effect on ORR, FDA conducted analyses on active ROM and PROs using the PROMIS-PF and BPI-30. The primary endpoint of ORR was supported by statistically significant improvements in active range of motion, patient-reported physical functioning, and patient-reported pain observed in the vimseltinib arm compared to the placebo arm at Week 25.
L72: Safety data supporting the proposed indication reflected exposure to vimseltinib in patients enrolled on the MOTION study (83 patients exposed to vimseltinib and 38 patients exposed to placebo). The most common adverse events for patients treated with vimseltinib included increased aspartate aminotransferase (AST), periorbital edema, fatigue, rash, increased cholesterol, peripheral edema, face edema, decreased neutrophils, decreased leukocytes, pruritus, and increased alanine aminotransferase (ALT).
L73: An additional 135 patients with TGCT or other solid tumors from study DCC-3014-01-001, an open-label, single arm, dose escalation and expansion study evaluating vimseltinib in patients with advanced solid tumors including TGCT were evaluated in the integrated summary of safety.
L74: Both pexidartinib and vimseltinib are kinase inhibitors that inhibit CSF1R. Given the important identified risk of severe hepatotoxicity observed with the use of pexidartinib, FDA performed a comprehensive analysis of the risk of hepatotoxicity associated with vimseltinib. FDA considered the need for a Boxed Warning and REMS based on a possible class effect and the identified risk for hepatotoxicity with use of pexidartinib. However, the safety data from the 253 patients who received at least one dose of vimseltinib in MOTION (including 35 patients who crossed over to receive vimseltinib during the open-label portion) or in the supportive study DCC-3014-01-001 did not identify a risk of serious or significant hepatotoxicity that would require a REMS or a boxed warning. While Grade 1 AST or ALT elevations were common, these did not lead to clinically significant hepatic toxicity or liver injury, with no cases of Hy's law observed. The Applicant argued that the mechanism of action for drug induced liver injury observed with pexidartinib (specifically, cholestatic toxicity) may be due to its unique biomolecular
L75: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L76: characteristics, including metabolites, rather than due to a class effect of agents targeting the CSF1R pathway. While the FDA reviewed the literature and concluded that the presence of unique metabolites might possibly explain pexidartinib-associated hepatotoxicity, the review team concluded that the nonclinical and clinical data could not fully rule out the risk for hepatotoxicity with this class of drugs including vimseltinib. Therefore, the review team included a section 5 warning regarding hepatotoxicity in product labeling (See Sections 5 and 8.2.5 for details). Additionally, a post-marketing requirement (PMR) registry study collecting data on liver function for an additional at least 100 patients dosed with vimseltinib in the postmarketing setting was agreed upon for this application. FDA also instituted an enhanced pharmacovigilance plan to capture hepatotoxicity in the postmarketing setting for patients not enrolled on the registry (See Sections 8.2.5 and 13 for details).
L77: Additional warnings and precautions included in section 5 of product labeling are embryo-fetal toxicity, allergic reactions to FD&amp;C Yellow No.5 and No.6 and increased creatinine without impact on renal function.
L78: A favorable risk:benefit assessment has been established for vimseltinib for the treatment of adult patients with symptomatic TGCT for which surgical resection will potentially cause worsening functional limitation based on the results of a single study, MOTION. Given the rarity of TGCT and small sample size, there are limited data to draw conclusions regarding subgroup analyses (i.e., race, age, sex, etc.) from this application.
L79: Postmarketing requirements for vimseltinib include the PMR registry study for hepatotoxicity described above, a PMR evaluating vimseltinib in participants with moderate hepatic impairment, a carcinogenicity study, and 3 PMRs to evaluate the potential for drug-drug interactions (with substrates of BCRP, OATP1B1/OATP1B3, and OCT2).
L80: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	TGCT is a rare neoplasm, with locally aggressive tumors that do not metastasize.
TGCT manifests as one or more tumors that grow in extra-articular synovial tissues or can be an intra-articular process involving the synovium and bursae.
Tumor mass typically expands in a slowly progressive manner.
TGCT is a serious disease that can result in significant morbidity.
L81: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L82: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Patients experience pain, stiffness, swelling and reduced ROM that impacts quality of life.
The annual incidence of TGCT in the United States is estimated to be 11 cases per million people. This is based on an estimate that 9.2 cases per million are localized TGCTs and 1.8 cases per million are diffuse-type TGCTs.
Recurrence rates are variable and have been reported to be 15% for localized disease and 55% for diffuse disease. Even higher rates of recurrence have been reported.

Current Treatment Options	Excision of tumor is the mainstay of treatment. Patients may require repeated surgical procedures, sometimes leading to loss of function, joint replacement, or amputation.
Pexidartinib is the only FDA-approved systemic therapy in this setting, demonstrating an ORR of 38% (95% CI: 27, 50), with 22/23 responders with \ge 6 months of follow-up maintaining response for \ge 6 months. Pexidartinib is available to patients only through implementation of a REMS due to severe and life-threatening liver toxicity, with an associated boxed warning.
Imatinib and nilotinib are listed in the NCCN guidelines for soft tissue sarcoma as useful in certain circumstances.
Radiation therapy may be reserved for those who are not surgical candidates and may reduce the risk of recurrence.
There is an unmet medical need for new effective systemic treatments for patients with TGCT. Due to the nature of TGCT tumors, complete surgical resection is often challenging and the risk of recurrence is variable and has been reported to be as high as 70%. The only approved drug for this condition is not curative and can cause life-threatening hepatotoxicity.

MOTION demonstrated a clinically meaningful and durable response in patients who received vimseltinib compared to placebo. Secondary endpoints including ROM and PROs were generally supportive of ORR.
ORR is an acceptable endpoint to support approval in this context. ORR results are supported by clinical outcome assessments including improvement in ROM and PROs. The submitted data meets the evidentiary standards for traditional approval in the proposed patient population.

Risk and Risk Management	The toxicity profile for vimseltinib includes increased AST, periorbital edema, fatigue, rash, increased cholesterol, peripheral edema, face edema, decreased neutrophils, decreased leukocytes, pruritus, and increased ALT. Toxicities were generally manageable with supportive care or dose modifications.
AST/ALT elevations in particular were almost all Grade 1, with no Grade 3 events.
Treatment with another CSF1R targeted drug, pexidartinib, confers a risk of hepatotoxicity. The potential risk of liver injury to patients receiving vimseltinib was thus a major review issue. Although low-
Severe hepatotoxicity was not observed in a total of 253 patients treated with vimseltinib, however hepatotoxicity is seen in patients treated with pexidartinib, another kinase inhibitor that inhibits CSF1R. Though the structure of pexidartinib and resultant metabolites may account for some of the hepatotoxicity (specifically cholestatic hepatotoxicity), nonclinical and clinical data could not fully rule out the risk for hepatotoxicity with vimseltinib.

L100: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L101: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L102: ## The Applicant's Position:
L103: Tenosynovial giant cell tumor (TGCT) is a rare tumor arising from the synovium of joints, bursae, and tendon sheaths (de St. Aubain Somerhausen and van de Rijn, 2013) caused by dysregulation of the colony stimulating factor 1 (CSF1) gene which results in overproduction of CSF1 and recruitment of CSF1 receptor (CSF1R)-dependent inflammatory cells into the affected joint (van der Heijden et al, 2023; West et al, 2006). The lesion can either present as a single nodule (localized form) or as multiple nodules (diffuse form) along a synovial layer or tendon sheath. The annual incidence rate of TGCT in the United States (US) is reported as approximately 11 per million (localized: 9.2 per million and diffuse TGCT: 1.8 per million) (Myers and Masi, 1980), with a more recent epidemiological study from the Netherlands reporting a higher worldwide incidence of 43 per million (digits: 29 per million; localized extremities: 10 per million; and diffuse TGCT: 4 per million) (Mastboom et al, 2017). Surgical resection is considered the first treatment option; however, patients may present with inoperable disease or may not be suitable candidates for surgery. Surgery for such patients could cause significant functional limitation or severe morbidity. In addition, relapse rates have been reported for up to 50% of diffuse-type TGCT patients, requiring repetitive surgical treatment that can lead to substantial damage to the affected joint and impaired quality of life (QoL) (Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016). As patients are typically diagnosed early in life (35 to 50 years old), limitations from increased morbidity can have a significant impact on QoL (Mastboom et al, 2018, Stacchiotti et al, 2023).
L104: Although TGCT is not life threatening, patients carry the burden of requiring anti-inflammatory and analgesic medications, including opioids, to alleviate the severe pain and debilitation. Additionally, for patients who undergo multiple surgeries, there is also the burden of rehabilitation and recovery after every procedure, which also contributes to associated morbidities. Thus, systemic disease targeted therapy is necessary for patients with inoperable or recurrent TGCT.
L105: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L106: FDA agrees with applicant's overall assessment. Recurrence rate for patients with localized TGCT is lower than patients with diffuse TGCT (dTGCT), and the reported rate of recurrence for patients with dTGCT has been reported to be as high as 70% (Stacchiotti 2023). In this rare tumor, little data is reported on the race and ethnicity of patients with TGCT. One study conducted in 83 patients with TGCT who received pexidartinib, showed that 65% of patients were White, 18% per Other. 8% were Black, and 1.2% were missing. There were no Native
L107: Americans or Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander reported. Most patients (69% of vimseltinib treated, 70% of placebo treated) had diffuse TGCT. In all patients, the most commonly affected joint was knee (68% in both vimseltinib and placebo treated), followed by ankle (11%, 15%) and hip (13%, 2.5%). Mean age at time of enrollment is similar in patients treated with vimseltinib (44, range: 20-78 years old) and placebo (42.5, range: 21-72)
L108: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L109: Surgery remains the recommended treatment for all forms of TGCT, when feasible. Localized TGCT can generally be surgically removed, but removal of diffuse TGCT may be challenging because the diffuse nature of the lesions may make full excision difficult (van der Heijden et al, 2023). Retrospective analysis of patients with newly diagnosed or first relapse TGCT was conducted to understand surgical outcomes and shows most patients (85%) with localized form of TGCT have a complete resection, while only 37% of patients with diffuse type of TGCT have a complete resection (Palmerini et al, 2015).
L110: Recurrence is observed in both forms of the disease following surgery. In localized TGCT, reported recurrence rate is approximately 10% after surgery (Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016; van der Heijden et al, 2023). Recurrence of localized TGCT is strongly associated with a negative outcome. Approximately 43% of patients who experience a relapse, even after a complete surgical resection, have a subsequent relapse within 5 years (Palmerini et al, 2015). Recurrence rates are considerably higher for diffuse TGCT, up to 55% (Ehrenstein et al, 2017; Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016) and a relapse-free survival rate of approximately 40% in 10 years (van der Heijden et al, 2023). Patients with recurring TGCT may require repetitive surgical treatment that can lead to substantial damage to the affected joint and impaired QoL (Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016; Mastboom et al, 2018). Eventually, some patients may require limb amputation for severe complications or present with metastatic disease (Gouin and Noailles, 2016; Staals et al, 2016). Multiple surgeries owing to recurrence may not be possible for patients who have significant risks associated with open surgery (eg, older patients, patients with comorbidities), or present with inoperable disease. Surgery for such patients could cause significant functional limitation or severe morbidity.
L111: Systemic therapies have been tested in patients with locally advanced or relapsed diffuse TGCT (Brahmi et al, 2016), including kinase inhibitors that non-selectively target the CSF1/CSF1R axis (eg, imatinib and nilotinib), and monoclonal antibodies targeting CSF1R expression (eg, emactuzumab, and cabiralizumab) (Stacchiotti et al, 2023; Gronchi et al, 2021). These kinase inhibitors have not been developed for the treatment of TGCT and have been accessed outside of approved labeling for the treatment of TGCT, and monoclonal antibodies are investigational. Best clinical practices have justified the use of systemic therapies in symptomatic TGCT based on the location and extent of the disease, particularly if disease progression or surgical intervention may result in joint damage and affect QoL (Stacchiotti et al, 2023).
L112: Pexidartinib (TURALIO®), a kinase inhibitor of CSF1R, cellular homolog of the feline sarcoma viral oncogene v-kit (KIT), and FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 (FLT3), was approved by the Food
L113: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L114: and Drug Administration (FDA) in 2019 for the treatment of adult patients with symptomatic TGCT associated with severe morbidity or functional limitations and not amenable to improvement with surgery. The approval was based on the results of the placebo-controlled, double-blind Phase 3 study (ENLIVEN) showing an objective response rate (ORR) of 38% for the pexidartinib arm over 0% in the placebo arm at study Week 25. However, pexidartinib can cause serious and potentially fatal liver injury and its use requires frequent liver tests for monitoring of liver toxicity. Pexidartinib's prescribing information includes a black box warning of hepatotoxicity and is only available to patients in the United States through a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) program (TURALIO USPI, 2023).
L115: A high unmet medical need remains for a selective CSF1R inhibitor with a more favorable risk/benefit profile.
L116: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L117: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of currently available treatments. In many patients, surgery is potentially curative, and surgery is often recommended as first-line treatment in patients for whom surgery would not worsen morbidity of functioning. Surgical options may include marginal excision with synovectomy, debulking surgery, total joint replacement and in rare cases, amputation. In patients for whom surgery is not an option, pexidartinib is the only FDA-approved drug; however, pexidartinib is only available through a REMS program and has a box warning due to risk of serious, potentially irreversible, liver injury, including vanishing bile duct syndrome. Pexidartinib was approved based on a multi-center, double-blind, placebo-controlled study which demonstrated an ORR per RECIST at Week 25 of 38% (95% CI: 27, 50). Twenty-two of the 23 patients who achieved a response and had been followed for at least six months had maintained the response for \ge 6 months. Additionally, patients who received pexidartinib experienced a statistically significant improvement in range of motion.
L118: Per NCCN guidelines, imatinib and nilotinib are other systemic therapies that are useful in certain circumstances. In certain circumstances, radiation can be considered for patients with diffuse or recurrent TGCT, though data does not currently support inclusion of radiotherapy as a standard treatment. Postsurgical external beam radiation has been shown to improve functional status and provide local control in some patients (Baniel 2023). In all patients with TGCT, active surveillance may be considered, and the risks and benefits of potential treatment must be evaluated for each individual patient. Patients with a low symptom burden and patients for whom surgery would likely not be curative and may increase morbidity may benefit from active surveillance.

L140: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L141: - In general, FDA agrees with Applicant's assessment with the following additions noted by the reviewer: Type C Meeting, March 3, 2020: FDA recommended the Applicant consider a study that includes an internal control and stated that FDA was open to a randomized controlled study with a provision for patients crossing over following a limited treatment course for patients randomized to placebo. FDA stated that patients must have surgical consultation prior to enrollment in MOTION, as surgery is potentially curative in some patients.
- Type C Meeting, July 22, 2020: FDA provided extensive feedback on COA and PRO elements. FDA agreed that proposed concepts of interest (ROM, physical function, pain, stiffness, swelling and joint instability) are important and relevant to the experience of patients with TGCT. FDA requested additional information on the proposed inclusion of qualitative data via the 15 PROMIS-PF measure, which Deciphera provided. FDA agreed with the use of PROMIS-PF and noted that the assessment may bias towards showing less of a response in patients with upper extremity TGCT. FDA agreed that the worst pain NRS was a content-valid item. FDA recommended that interpretation of worst stiffness and worst swelling be done with consideration for potential confounders and noted that qualitative data in RA suggests that patients may not be able to differentiate between stiffness, pain, and swelling. The Applicant proposed removal of the question regarding joint instability, and FDA agreed.
L142: - Type C Meeting, August 14, 2020: FDA agreed to the key design elements proposed for MOTION and agreed to the study endpoints, sample size, randomization ratio, study duration and statistical methods. FDA noted that hepatic function criteria in the proposed MOTION study (AST, ALT, bilirubin \le 1 times upper limit of normal) are more stringent than in DCC-3014-01-001 (\le 1.5 times upper limit of normal), the supportive study.
- Type B Meeting, January 29, 2021: FDA agreed the Phase 3 study design may be appropriate to support future approval of vimseltinib for the treatment of symptomatic TGCT(b)(4). FDA noted that the ability of the proposed study to support a marketing application would depend on the observed ORR and DOR. FDA agreed that the primary and secondary endpoints were appropriate to capture clinically meaningful outcomes. FDA acknowledged the planned safety database size of approximately 400 patients, including 200 oncology patients and 200 patients in clinical pharmacology studies and stated this may be considered adequate to support an NDA provided that no unusual toxicities were identified.
- Type B, pNDA Meeting, January 16, 2024: FDA additionally noted that efficacy data for the original and the complete NDA should include a minimum of six months of follow-up from the date of the initial confirmed response for all responders. The Applicant agreed to submit updated efficacy data based on a second data cutoff date of February 2024 to provide additional durability and follow-up data. FDA agreed that the additional DOR data would be used to support labeling, but the additional ORR data would not. The Applicant provided information regarding planned safety analyses.
L143: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L144: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)

Inspections were performed by OSI for Dr. Gelderblom (site 431), Dr. Blay (Site 402), and the imaging Contract Research Organization (CRO) (b)(4). The inspection of the CRO did not reveal any discrepancies, regulatory violations, or Good Clinical Practice noncompliance. The inspections for Drs. Gelderblom and Blay did not find any significant concerns regarding the study conduct, data integrity, GCP, or regulatory compliance. Based on these inspections, MOTION appears to have been conducted adequately by the study sponsor and the data generated by the inspected clinical investigators and the imaging CRO and submitted by the Applicant appear acceptable in support of the proposed indication.
L146: ## 4.2. Product Quality

L152: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L153: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L154: Vimseltinib is a small molecule kinase inhibitor of colony-stimulating factor 1 receptor (CSF1R). The established pharmacologic class is kinase inhibitor.
L155: **Figure 1** Chemical Structure of Vimseltinib
L156: *(Excerpted from Applicant's Submission)*
L157: ## *Target Background*
L158: Colony stimulating factor 1 receptor (CSF1R), also known as CD115 (Cluster of Differentiation 115) or macrophage colony-stimulating factor receptor (M-CSFR), is a tyrosine kinase transmembrane receptor encoded by the human CSF1R gene (also known as c-FMS). CSF1R signaling is involved in macrophage and osteoclast development and differentiation. In tenosynovial giant cell tumors, colony stimulating factor 1 (CSF-1) is overexpressed. Overexpression of CSF1 and excess signaling through CSF1R may enhance tumor-associated macrophage (TAM)-mediated tumor growth, angiogenesis, and immunosuppression.
L159: ## *Pharmacology*
L160: Vimseltinib inhibited CSF1R with an IC50 of 2.8 nM. In vitro, vimseltinib inhibited autophosphorylation and phosphorylation of CSF1R in THP-1 cells (a cell line used to study monocyte and macrophage function) and ERK signaling in whole blood monocytes. Vimseltinib had anti-tumor activity, inhibiting CSF1R-dependent proliferation in vitro in M-NFS-60 myelogenous leukemia cells. In an in vivo mouse model, administration of vimseltinib reduced levels of CSF1R-dependent cFOS transcription.
L161: ## *ADME/PK*
L162: A single IV dose of 1 mg/kg vimseltinib had 73% CNS permeability in Sprague Dawley rats.
L163: ## *General toxicology*
L164: Repeat-dose toxicology studies were conducted in rats (4-weeks, 13-weeks, and 26-weeks) and dogs (4-weeks, 13-weeks, and 39-weeks). The indication did not warrant repeat-dose toxicology studies of more than 3 months duration.
L165: Rats exhibited similar findings across studies:
L166: - swollen head and limbs
- decreases in RBC-related parameters (hemocrit, hemoglobin, erythrocytes), lymphocytes, calcium, and phosphate
- increases in AST, ALT, glutamate dehydrogenase (GLDH), fibrinogen, reticulocytes, platelets, and neutrophils
- notable adverse histopathology in the bone, teeth, kidneys (chronic progressive nephropathy), and blood vessel degeneration (fibrinoid necrosis with presence of inflammatory cells) in multiple tissues.
- additional findings in rats relevant to reproductive effects are described below under *Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity*
L167: Rats were more sensitive to the effects of vimseltinib compared to dogs.
L168: Dogs were dosed higher in the 4-week study (up to 25 mg/kg/day) vs. the 13-week and 39-week studies (up to 8 mg/kg/day) and exhibited more severe toxicities at higher doses. Considering all three studies, findings included:
L169: - decreases in phosphate, calcium, and APTT
- increases in AST, ALT, GLDH, creatine kinase, fibrinogen, reticulocytes, and platelets
- edema in the skin, skin de-pigmentation (females only), excessive lacrimation, and epididymal mineralization
L170: The vimseltinib toxicity profile is characterized by inflammation, hematologic, hepatic, renal, bone, and gastrointestinal toxicities, and is generally consistent with CSF1R inhibition. Findings in animals indicate vimseltinib has the potential to cause hepatotoxicity, which may be secondary to inflammation. Another small molecule kinase inhibitor of CSF1R is pexidartinib. Literature (Qin et al., 2023) suggests that the mechanism of action for pexidartinib-mediated liver injury may be due to its unique biomolecular characteristics, including metabolites, rather than due to a class effect alone. While plausible, the totality of the nonclinical data for vimseltinib do not fully rule out the risk for hepatotoxicity in humans.
L171: ## *General toxicology findings reflected in USPI sections 8.4 and 13.2*
L172: In a 26-week repeat-dose general toxicology study in rats, chronic progressive nephropathy occurred at vimseltinib doses of \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day (approximately 6 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). Degeneration of blood vessels (perivascular inflammation and necrosis of arteries and arteriole walls) occurred in multiple tissues at 5 mg/kg/day vimseltinib (approximately 12 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC).
L173: Rats administered vimseltinib at \ge 2.5 mg/kg had physeal thickening and decay of the incisors and molars. Bone and tooth toxicities occurred at exposures approximately 8 times the
L174: recommended dose based on AUC. These findings are relevant to pediatric use.
L175: In a 39-week repeat-dose general toxicology study in dogs, skin depigmentation in the head and legs occurred at vimseltinib doses of \ge 4 mg/kg/day corresponding to exposures lower than the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC.
L176: ## Genotoxicity
L177: Vimseltinib was not mutagenic in the bacterial reverse mutation assay (Ames). In an in vitro micronucleus assay, vimseltinib increased micronuclei after a 24-hour incubation in the absence of metabolic activation. In vivo, vimseltinib administered to rats at doses up to 200 mg/kg/day did not increase bone marrow micronucleated polychromatic erythrocytes, nor did vimseltinib increase liver DNA strand breaks. The overall assessment was that vimseltinib was negative for genotoxicity.
L178: ## Carcinogenicity
L179: In a 6-month transgenic mouse carcinogenicity study at doses up to 12.5 mg/kg/day, vimseltinib was negative for carcinogenic findings. The Applicant is currently conducting a 104-week carcinogenicity study in Sprague Dawley rats to be submitted as a PMR.
L180: ## Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L181: In a rat embryo-fetal development study, pregnant female rats were dosed once daily during the period of organogenesis (gestational days 6 to 17) at doses of 2.5, 5, or 15 mg/kg/day. Structural abnormalities (skeletal variations) occurred at \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day (approximately 3 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). Additional structural abnormalities (cardiac malformations) were observed at the highest dose of 15 mg/kg/day (approximately 23 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC).
L182: Based on data from animal studies and its mechanism of action, vimseltinib can cause fetal harm. Females of reproductive potential and males with female partners of reproductive potential are recommended to use effective contraception during treatment with vimseltinib and for 1 month after the last dose. The duration of contraception is based on 5 times the clinical elimination half-life of 6 days.
L183: There are no data on the presence of vimseltinib or its metabolites in either human or animal milk or its effects on a breastfed child or on milk production. Because of the potential for serious adverse reactions in the breastfed child, women are recommended not to breastfeed during treatment with vimseltinib and for 1 month after the last dose.
L184: Based on findings from animal studies, vimseltinib may impair fertility.
L185: In a fertility and early embryonic development study, male rats were administered 1, 2.5, or 5 mg/kg/day of vimseltinib starting 10 weeks before cohabitation, during cohabitation with
L186: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L187: untreated females, and at least 2 weeks post-cohabitation. Lower epididymal and testes weights were observed at 5 mg/kg/day (approximately 12 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC). There were no treatment-related effects on mating, fertility, or sperm parameters at any dose tested. Female rats were administered 2.5, 5, or 10 mg/kg/day of vimseltinib 2 weeks prior to cohabitation with untreated males and during cohabitation until gestational day 7. Although there were no treatment-related effects on mating or estrous cycles, vimseltinib administered daily resulted in post-implantation loss at 10 mg/kg/day in female rats (approximately 20 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC).
L188: In a 26-week repeat-dose general toxicology study, recovery male rats that were administered 2.5 or 5 mg/kg/day had moderate to marked reductions in sperm and marked testicular atrophy (1 of 5 and 2 of 5 animals, respectively) corresponding to approximately 6 and 12 times the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC, respectively. In a 39-week repeat-dose general toxicology study, minimal to moderate epididymal mineralization occurred in male dogs administered \ge 4 mg/kg/day corresponding to exposures lower than the exposure at the recommended dose based on AUC.

L197: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L198: The Applicant's Position:
L199: Primary pharmacology
L200: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L201: Vimseltinib selectively and potently inhibits CSF1R in recombinant kinase assays and in cellular assays with CSF1R-expressing cells, including monocytic cell lines, osteoclast precursors, and monocytes from freshly drawn whole blood samples. In vivo, pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) analysis of vimseltinib inhibition of CSF1R signaling demonstrates potent and durable inhibition of CSF1R. Vimseltinib depletes tumor-associated macrophages (TAMs) in an immunocompetent colorectal cancer model. In a prostate cancer model, wherein primary tumor growth results in bone invasion and osteoclast-mediated bone degradation, vimseltinib treatment demonstrates statistically significant protection of bone degradation. DP-7005, a metabolite of vimseltinib, is an inhibitor of CSF1R kinase in enzyme and cell-based assays.
L202: ## In Vitro Pharmacology
L203: Vimseltinib is a potent inhibitor of CSF1R kinase in vitro in both recombinant enzyme and cell based assays. Vimseltinib inhibits CSF1-stimulated CSF1R autophosphorylation in a monocytic cell line, inhibits cellular proliferation of a monocytic cell line dependent on CSF1R signaling, inhibits CSF1-stimulated osteoclast differentiation, and decreases macrophage-mediated tumor cell migration. Vimseltinib also inhibits CSF1R signaling in monocytes in an in vitro human whole blood assay.

L211: ## In Vivo Pharmacology
L212: Vimseltinib was well tolerated in all mouse pharmacology studies. In a PK/PD model, vimseltinib significantly inhibited CSF1-stimulated expression of cellular homolog of the Finkel Biskis-Jinkins murine osteogenic sarcoma virus oncogene (cFOS) mRNA for &gt;24 h postdose at all doses, as low as 3.75 mg/kg PO, after a single dose. After dosing at 10 mg/kg orally for 6 days, vimseltinib inhibited CSF1-stimulated cFOS expression by &gt;80% for &gt;48 h after the last dose (Figure 3 and Table 4). After dosing at 3 mg/kg, vimseltinib inhibited cFOS expression by 90% or greater for 12 h, and approximately 50% at 24 h and 48 h. The 24 h area under the concentration time/curve (AUC) for the 3 mg/kg group was 49,314 ng*h/mL (Table 5). The half maximally effective concentration (EC_{50}) was 430 ng/mL and the eighty percent maximally effective concentration (EC_{80}) was 1,700 ng/mL.

L235: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L236: ## The Applicant's Position:
L237: In a large panel of kinase activity assays, vimseltinib exhibits &gt;100-fold selectivity for CSF1R kinase versus 298 other human kinases, including its closely related family members KIT, platelet-derived growth factor receptor alpha (PDGFR\alpha), PDGFR beta (PDGFR\beta), and FLT3 (DCC-3014-02-0002, DCC-3014-02-0003, and DCC-3014-02-0031, eCTD 4.2.1.2; Figure 7). Vimseltinib was evaluated versus a battery of 135 receptors, ion channels, and enzymes (DCC-3014-02-0010, eCTD 4.2.1.2). Among the battery of 104 receptor binding assays profiled, vimseltinib inhibited MT3 (ML2) (agonist radioligand) by 68.2% when evaluated at 10 \muM concentration. All other receptor binding assays were inhibited &lt;25% by vimseltinib. Among the battery of 31 enzyme and uptake assays, vimseltinib exhibited 86.2% inhibition of ABL kinase, 42.8% inhibition of FYN kinase, and 90.6% inhibition of LYN kinase when evaluated at 10 \muM concentration. These kinases were further tested, wherein IC50 values of 2,200 nM, &gt;3,300 nM,

## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)

L247: ## Safety Pharmacology
L248: ## The Applicant's Position:
L249: Vimseltinib was assessed in nonclinical safety pharmacology studies including evaluation of neurobehavioral effects and evaluation of impact on cardiovascular parameters and respiratory function. There were no effects of vimseltinib on central nervous system (CNS) function in rats at single doses up to 30 mg/kg, the highest dose tested. A mildly higher tidal volume was noted in rats given 30 mg/kg in the respiratory safety pharmacology study, without an effect on respiratory rate or minute volume. The 30 mg/kg/day dose exceeds the severely toxic dose in 10% of the evaluation cohort (STD10) determined in the definitive Good Laboratory Practice (GLP) compliant 28-day rat study. In the dog cardiovascular (CV) safety pharmacology study, no vimseltinib-related qualitative electrocardiogram (ECG) effects or quantitative changes in PR interval, QRS duration, QT interval, QT interval corrected by Fridericia's formula (QTcF) interval, heart rate (HR), arterial pressure, or body temperature were seen at doses up to 15 mg/kg. In the in vitro human ether-à-go-go-related gene (hERG) study, the half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC50) values for inhibition of the hERG potassium current by vimseltinib and DP-7005 could not be calculated due to 17% or less inhibition at the maximum concentrations that could be tested at the solubility limits of the two compounds (30 µM). Based
L250: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L251: on these results, vimseltinib appears unlikely to have significant CNS, respiratory, or cardiovascular safety pharmacology liabilities in humans. Comprehensive data from Deciphera clinical trials support the nonclinical data and conclusions indicating no safety concerns related to CNS, respiratory, or cardiovascular effects.

L258: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L259: ## The Applicant's Position:
L260: The absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) of vimseltinib and its active metabolite, DP-7005, were characterized in a range of in vitro and in vivo studies to support the proposed clinical indication and dosing regimen. The absorption of vimseltinib has been characterized in an extensive program of single-dose PK studies and repeated-dose toxicokinetic (TK) studies in mouse, rat, dog, and monkey. The distribution of vimseltinib was evaluated in a quantitative whole-body autoradiography study and vimseltinib's ability to penetrate the CNS was assessed in rats. Plasma protein binding was evaluated in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and humans. The characterization of vimseltinib's metabolites were assessed in vitro with mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human hepatocytes and in vivo in dogs and bile duct-cannulated (BDC) rats. Metabolic stability was evaluated in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human liver microsomes and hepatocytes while cytochrome P450 enzyme (CYP) reaction phenotyping was assessed in human liver microsomes and recombinant human CYP enzymes. CYP induction and inhibition were evaluated using human hepatocytes and liver microsomes, respectively. The excretion of vimseltinib was evaluated in dogs and in bile-duct cannulated rats. The potential for vimseltinib to affect transporter activity was evaluated in vitro.
L261: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L262: ## Absorption
L263: The PK parameters of vimseltinib following oral and IV administration of vimseltinib in male Sprague Dawley rats (DCC-3014-03-0012) and male beagle dogs (DCC-3014-03-0014) are provided in Table 7. PK parameters in rats following oral administration were similar to IV, with the exception that T_{max} occurred at 2.67-24 hours, depending on dose level, instead of immediately after infusion. The PK of DP-7005 was evaluated in dog studies in addition to vimseltinib after dog microsomal studies indicated substantial formation of the metabolite DP-7005.
L264: A key finding from the single dose oral PK studies in rats (Table 8) was a plateau in vimseltinib exposures between 100 and 300 mg/kg, which was used to inform dose selection in repeat-dose toxicology studies.

L277: ## Distribution
L278: Plasma Protein Binding	An in vitro distribution study characterized the plasma protein binding of vimseltinib in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human plasma. Percent bound values ranged from 95.4% in monkey, 96.4% in dogs and 99.3% in rat plasma. Percent bound in human plasma was determined to be 96.5 (DCC-3014-03-0002, eCTD 4.2.2.3).
L279: CNS Penetrability	An in vivo distribution study to assess CNS penetrability was performed in rats. Vimseltinib exhibited a brain:plasma ratio of 0.73, indicating a significant 73% CNS penetrability after systemic IV dosing (DCC-3014-03-0015, eCTD 4.2.2.3).
L280: Tissue Distribution	After oral dosing to male Sprague Dawley and Long Evans rats, [14C]-vimseltinib-derived radioactivity was widely distributed to almost all tissues by the first collection time point (0.5 hours postdose). Radioactivity was cleared from most tissues by 168 hours postdose, but still quantifiable in the meninges, eye uveal tract, eye(s), and eye vitreous humor at 672 hours (DCC-3014-03-0022, eCTD 4.2.2.4).
L281: ## Metabolism
L282: Metabolite Profile	The metabolite profiles of [14C]-vimseltinib were evaluated in vitro using mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human hepatocytes and microsomes (DCC-3014-03-0008 and DCC-3014-03-0023, both eCTD 4.2.2.4). Metabolism was species-dependent. [14C]-vimseltinib was metabolized in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human hepatocytes and microsomes via oxidation, N-dealkylation, and dehydrogenation. No Phase II metabolites were detected. Up to 7 metabolites were tentatively identified/characterized in hepatocytes and microsomes, in addition to unchanged vimseltinib. Metabolites detected in human hepatocytes and microsomes were also found in other species. Metabolic profiling as part of the human AME
L283: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L284: study (DCC-3014-01-003, eCTD 5.3.3.1) determined that primary metabolism occurred by oxidation, N-demethylation, and N-dealkylation; secondary biotransformation pathways included N-demethylation, dehydrogenation, and oxidation.
Metabolite identification studies in hepatocytes were also completed (DCC-3014-03-0008). Of the 24 identified metabolites observed across all species (mouse, rat, dog, monkey, human), only four were present at levels high enough for UV detection in the hepatocyte incubations, and only one (C6, DP-7005) was observed by UV in the human hepatocyte incubations. No human-specific metabolites were observed in the study, but neither mouse rat, dog, nor monkey formed all the vimseltinib metabolites observed in human hepatocyte incubations. The rat and dog hepatocytes between them provided coverage of all the observed human metabolites except component C19. This metabolite coverage supported the use of rats and dogs as appropriate toxicological species.
The metabolism of [14C]-vimseltinib after a single IV bolus or oral administration was evaluated in dogs (DCC-3014-03-0021, eCTD 4.2.2.4). Vimseltinib and DP-7005 were the major circulating components in plasma after an IV or oral dose. Vimseltinib underwent biotransformation and was primarily metabolized via N-dealkylation to yield the major metabolite DP-7005 after both oral and IV administration (DCC-3014-03-0022, eCTD 4.2.2.4). Minor biotransformation pathways included oxidation, dehydrogenation, and glucuronidation.
While the metabolite DP-7005 was minimally formed in rat and human hepatocytes, it was formed to a much greater extent in dog and monkey hepatocytes (DCC-3014-03-0008). Metabolite DP-7005 was experimentally measured in the plasma of both rats and dogs after single or multiple oral dose vimseltinib PK studies, although its appearance in rat plasma was minimal (approximately 2% compared to exposure of parent vimseltinib) consistent with its minimal formation in microsomal preparations. The minimal appearance of DP-7005 in rat contrasts with the proportion formed in dogs after oral administration of vimseltinib, wherein metabolite DP-7005 exposure dominated by up to approximately 5-fold compared to parent vimseltinib in multiple dose studies (DCC-3014-04-0005, eCTD 4.2.3.2). Metabolite DP-7005, like vimseltinib, is a CSF1R inhibitor (IC_{50} = 65 nM). In the human AME study, the metabolite-to-parent molar AUC ratio for DP-7005 was 0.05 confirming minimal formation of DP-7005 in humans after a single oral dose (DCC-3014-01-003 CSR).

Metabolic Stability	
The overall extent of vimseltinib stability in hepatocytes from highest to lowest followed the rank order mouse~rat~dog~human>monkey. Half-lives in human and mouse hepatocyte preparations were equal or greater than 4 hours, as no substrate loss was observed after 240 minutes of incubation with mouse and human hepatocytes (DCC-3014-03-0003, eCTD 4.2.2.4).

Excretion	
The excretion of [14C]-vimseltinib was evaluated after a single administration to rats via oral gavage. Most of the [14C]-vimseltinib-derived radioactivity after oral administration to both intact males and females was excreted primarily in feces within 96 hours after dosing. Excretion profiles were similar between males and females, suggesting no sex differences. Following oral dosing to BDC male rats, a significant percentage of radioactivity was eliminated in the bile after oral dosing, indicating the biliary excretion was a significant route of elimination of absorbed [14C]vimseltinib-derived radioactivity. Fecal excretion was the major pathway for elimination of [14C]-vimseltinib-derived radioactivity in intact and BDC rats after oral dosing (DCC-3014-03-0022).
L285: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L286: The excretion of [^{14}\text{C}]-vimseltinib was evaluated after a single IV or oral administration to dogs. Fecal and/or biliary excretion were the major pathway for elimination of [^{14}\text{C}]-vimseltinib-derived radioactivity in male and female dogs (DCC-3014-03-0021).
Elimination of vimseltinib in humans occurred by metabolism and urinary and fecal excretions. In human AME study DCC-3014-01-003, when extrapolated to infinity, 37.5% of the administered ^{14}\text{C}-vimseltinib dose was excreted in urine and 42.9% in feces.

Drug-Drug Interaction
CYP Reaction Phenotyping	
In reaction phenotyping studies (time course studies in microsomal preparations and recombinant human CYP enzyme incubations; DCC-3014-03-0007, eCTD 4.2.2.4) vimseltinib disappearance ranged from zero to 10% over a 120 minute incubation period when human liver microsomes were incubated with vimseltinib at various concentrations (0.1, 1 and 10 \mu\text{M}). Substrate loss was 6% or less in the absence of microsomal protein, and no loss occurred in the absence of the NADPH-generating system at all three concentrations of vimseltinib (Module 2.6.5, Table 39). Incubations of vimseltinib (0.1 and 1 \mu\text{M}) were performed in a panel of recombinant human CYP enzymes (20 pmol CYP/mL; 30 minutes) (Module 2.6.5, Table 39). Loss of vimseltinib (0.1 \mu\text{M}) ranged from 2.8 to 22.3% in incubations with the panel of recombinant human CYP enzymes. The negative control incubations, control bactosomes and reductase control, resulted in 0.6 and 3.9% substrate loss, respectively. Loss of vimseltinib (1.0 \mu\text{M}) ranged from zero to 18.6% in incubations with the panel of recombinant human CYP enzymes. The negative control incubations (control bactosomes and reductase control) resulted in 3.1 and 7.5% substrate loss, respectively.

CYP Induction and Inhibition	
In vitro, both vimseltinib and DP-7005 exhibited \text{IC}_{50} values >40 \mu\text{M} for all CYP isoforms studied (40 \mu\text{M} was the highest concentration studied for DP-7005), and \text{IC}_{50} values >100 \mu\text{M} were exhibited by vimseltinib for five out of seven CYP isoforms (DCC-3014-03-0001, eCTD 4.2.2.4). Additionally, neither vimseltinib nor DP-7005 exhibited time-dependent or metabolism-dependent inhibition of any CYP isoform, demonstrating little to no risk for time- or metabolism-dependent inhibition of CYP isoforms.
Vimseltinib demonstrated low to minimal risk for CYP3A4 or CYP1A2 enzyme induction, and a slight risk for CYP2B6 induction at anticipated clinical exposures (<10 \mu\text{M}; DCC-3014-03-0009, eCTD 4.2.2.4).

Transporters	
Vimseltinib is a moderate to weak inhibitor of various human efflux transporters (\text{IC}_{50} values from 4.35 to 51.5) (DCC-3014-03-0006, eCTD 4.2.2.6). Two transporters, BCRP and OCT2, were inhibited by vimseltinib with \text{IC}_{50} values of less than 1 \mu\text{M} (0.556 and 0.456 \mu\text{M}, respectively).
The efflux ratio of vimseltinib across MDCKII-MDR1 (P-gp) cells was 2.62 and decreased to 1.48 in the presence of a P-gp inhibitor (valspodar). Vimseltinib was not a substrate of BCRP, BSEP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3 OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE-2K. DP-7005 was also not a substrate of MATE1 or MATE2-K.

L292: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L293: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L294: ## The Applicant's Position:
L295: The toxicological profile of vimseltinib has been characterized in an extensive program of repeated-dose toxicity studies of up to 26 weeks in rats and 39 weeks in dogs, a standard battery of genetic toxicity studies, carcinogenicity studies in transgenic mice and rats (ongoing), and reproductive and development toxicity studies in rats and rabbits. In addition, an in vitro assessment of the potential for vimseltinib to produce phototoxicity was performed. Pivotal toxicology studies were conducted in compliance with current International Conference on Harmonisation (ICH) Harmonised Tripartite Guidelines, generally accepted procedures for the testing of pharmaceutical compounds, and in concordance with the United States FDA GLP regulations, 21 CFR Part 58.
L296: In repeat dose toxicity studies of up to 26 weeks in rats, there were findings of swollen head and/or limbs, and abnormal teeth at doses of 1 mg/kg/day. The dental effects at doses of 5 mg/kg/day in male rats were associated with lower food consumption and reduced body weight. Chronic progressive nephropathy occurred in animals receiving \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day. Degeneration of blood vessels in multiple tissues and increased physis thickness was observed in rats receiving 5 mg/kg/day.
L297: In repeat dose toxicity studies of up to 39 weeks in dogs, there were findings of periocular swelling, epiphora, excessive lacrimation and discolored and depigmented skin on the head and legs (which were swollen and wrinkled [head only]) were observed for animals administered 4 and 8 mg/kg/day. All observations showed complete or partial recovery by the end of the recovery phase at 4 mg/kg/day, while observations of wrinkled skin, excessive lacrimation, and skin de-pigmentation (females only) persisted throughout the recovery phase at 8 mg/kg/day. Vimseltinib-related increased body weight and body weight gain were noted during the dosing phase for animals administered 8 mg/kg/day; however, during the recovery phase, animals previously administered 8 mg/kg/day were observed with body weight loss and clinical observations of decreased activity, thin appearance, fever, and hunched posture (which necessitated veterinary examination and/or treatment). Microscopic findings included slight to moderate edema in the skin collected from the head of animals administered 8 mg/kg/day, which correlated with macroscopic observations of swollen head and subcutaneous tissue having a gelatinous appearance. Based on the veterinary intervention and body weight loss noted during the recovery phase for animals administered 8 mg/kg/day, this dose level was considered adverse.

The broken and discolored teeth in males at 5.0 mg/kg/day were associated with decreased body weight gain and lower food consumption.

L300: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L301: Died or Sacrificed Moribund	
One toxicity male administered 2.5 mg/kg/day, a toxicity female administered 5.0 mg/kg/day, and a toxicokinetic female administered 5.0 mg/kg/day were sacrificed in a moribund condition on Days 166, 162, or 142 of the dosing phase, due to vimseltinib-related clinical observations of foot/footpad inflammation, skin findings (epidermal acanthosis and serocellular crust), decreased activity, and poor, unkempt haircoat and piloerection. One toxicity female administered 5.0 mg/kg/day died due to a procedural error while trimming overgrown lower incisors; this death was not vimseltinib-related.

Body Weight and Food Consumption	
Reversible, increased body weight and body weight gain were noted in males administered \le 2.5 mg/kg/day and females administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day.

Ophthalmoscopy	
No significant treatment-related findings

Hematology	Non-reversible, mildly to moderately, dose independent, higher WBC and absolute neutrophil count and globulin concentration and lower A:G ratio, accompanied by higher total protein concentration (reversible) was observed in animals administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day.
Reversible, lower calcium concentration (protein bound) was observed in rats administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day (attributed to lower albumin concentrations).
Reversible, minimally to mildly lower red blood cell count was observed in rats administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day.
Reversible, minimally to mildly lower indicators of red cell mass and absolute reticulocyte counts was observed in rats administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day.
Minimally higher MCV and RDW with minimally lower MCHC was observed in rats administered 5.0 mg/kg/day; minimally and dose-dependent higher RDW in rats administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day.
Reversible and minimally to mildly higher platelet counts were observed in rats administered 5.0 mg/kg/day.
Reversible, mildly lower lymphocyte counts was observed in rats administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day.

Clinical Chemistry	Non-reversible, minimally to mildly lower albumin concentration was observed in rats administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day or 5.0 mg/kg/day.
Non-reversible, dose-dependent, minimally to mildly higher globulin concentration and A:G ratio was observed in rats administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day
Reversible, minimally to mildly higher total protein concentrations was observed in rats administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day.
Reversible, minimally to mildly lower calcium concentrations was observed in rats administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day
Reversible, dose-dependent, mildly to moderately higher ALT and AST aminotransferase was observed in rats administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day.
Reversible, minimally lower inorganic phosphorus concentrations was observed in rats administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day and higher cholesterol concentration observed in females administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day and 5.0 mg/kg/day.

Urinalysis	Reversible, minimally higher urine specific gravity was observed in rats administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day associated with minimally lower urine volume noted in rats administered 5.0 mg/kg/day.
L302: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L303: Non-reversible and higher incidence and magnitude of positive protein reactions was observed in females administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day.
Reversible, hyaline casts was observed in one male and three females administered 5.0 mg/kg/day and one female administered 1.0 mg/kg/day.

Organ Weight	Reversible, mean organ weight effects included increased thyroid and parathyroid weights in rats administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day; and increased adrenal gland and liver weights in females administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day and decreased thymus weights in males administered 5.0 mg/kg/day. Higher mean spleen weights persisted in a few individual males and females administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day and \ge 5.0 mg/kg/day, respectively.

Microscopic Observations	Vimseltinib-related microscopic findings of degeneration/necrosis of vessels (at slight to moderate severity) in multiple tissues and increased physis thickness (moderate severity) were observed in rats administered 5.0 mg/kg/day, while CPN (moderate to severe severity) occurred in animals administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day, and degeneration/necrosis and dental dysplasia (minimal to slight) of molar teeth occurred in animals administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day; these microscopic findings were all considered adverse.
Partial reversibility of degeneration/necrosis of vessels and dental dysplasia and degeneration/necrosis of molars at recovery.
Observations of increased physis thickness and mixed cell inflammation in the swollen foot/footpad persisted at a similar severity following recovery.

L305: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L306: In the 28-day repeat-dose rat toxicology study (Study No. 8332976), vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 5, 15, or 30 mg/kg/day. Findings included:
L307: - decreases in RBC mass, lymphocytes, calcium, and phosphate
- increases in AST (up to a 230%), ALT (up to a 500%), glutamate dehydrogenase (GLDH) (up to a 180%), total protein, globulin, fibrinogen, platelets, and neutrophils.
L308: Vimseltinib-related microscopic findings included:
L309: - hypocellularity in bone marrow, necrosis/apoptosis in lymphoid tissues (lymph nodes, spleen)
- hypertrophy of zonal fasciculate and individual cell necrosis in adrenal
- acute inflammation in kidney, apoptosis/necrosis with infiltrating neutrophils in pylorus
- individual cell apoptosis/necrosis in stomach, individual cell apoptosis/necrosis in lamina propria of duodenum
L310: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L311: - hypertrophy of the physis and primary spongiosa in femur
- no liver pathology was noted
L312: Overall, the 28-day study identified signals for inflammation, hematologic, hepatic, renal, bone, and gastrointestinal toxicities.
L313: In the 13-week repeat-dose rat toxicology study (Study No. 8403647-1) with a 4-week recovery phase, vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 1.876, 3.75, or 7.5 mg/kg/day. Findings were similar to the 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study and included increases in AST, ALT, fibrinogen, platelets, and neutrophils and decreases in red cell mass, calcium, phosphate, and reticulocytes. Histopathology data are tabulated in Section 19.3.
L314: In the 26-week repeat-dose rat toxicology study (Study No. 8413878) with a 4-week recovery phase, vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 1, 2.5, or 5 mg/kg/day. Findings were similar to the 28-day and 13-week repeat-dose toxicology study.
L315: Clinical pathology findings are supportive of an inflammatory state, as well as risk for hepatotoxicity and bone-related findings.

L318: Administration of vimseltinib was associated with decreased calcium and phosphate. The Applicant suggests that the decreased calcium is a result of reduced serum albumin levels as most serum calcium is bound to albumin; however, the levels of calcium and phosphate return to that of the controls during recovery while serum albumin does not.
L319: The bone, kidney, and small intestine play a role in serum calcium and phosphate homeostasis. Administration of vimseltinib was associated with chronic progressive nephropathy and physeal thickening of the sternum and femur. Taken together these data suggest that the observed decreases in calcium are unlikely due to reduced serum albumin.
L320: In the following histopathology tables, the findings of degeneration/necrosis largely correspond to vessel findings. Vimseltinib-related degeneration/necrosis of vessels occurred in multiple tissues/organs of animals administered \ge 2.5 mg/kg/day. Vessel findings were characterized by fibrinoid necrosis of variably sized vessel (primarily artery or arteriole) walls, often with cellular debris and/or variable numbers of inflammatory cells within the wall and perivascular inflammatory cell infiltrates.

L336: ## The Applicant's Position:
L337: **Table 10** **39-Week Dog Study**
L338: Study Number:	DCC-3014-04-0013, eCTD 4.2.3.2
Study Title:	A 39-Week Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study with DCC-3014 in Beagle Dogs with a 4-Week Recovery Phase
Key Study Findings:	Vimseltinib was well tolerated during the dosing phase of 2, 4, or 8 mg/kg/day. The NOAEL for vimseltinib was 4 mg/kg/day.
Beagle dogs showed periocular swelling, epiphora, excessive lacrimation and discolored and depigmented skin on the head and legs (\ge4 mg/kg/day). All observations showed complete or partial recovery at \ge4 mg/kg/day while observations of wrinkled skin, excessive lacrimation, and skin de-pigmentation (females only) persisted throughout the recovery phase at 8 mg/kg/day.

L339: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L340: Findings	
Died or Sacrificed Moribund	All animals survived to scheduled necropsy.
Body Weight and Food Consumption	Reversible, increased body weight and body weight gain were observed in dogs administered 8 mg/kg/day. No vimseltinib-related alterations in food consumption were noted at any dose level.
Ophthalmoscopy	Reversible, periocular swelling and epiphora (partially reversible) were observed in dogs administered 8 mg/kg/day.
ECG	No significant treatment-related findings
Hematology	Hematologic findings were mild in magnitude, generally non-progressive, lacked microscopic correlates, with reversibility during recovery.
Clinical Chemistry	Clinical chemistry findings were mild in magnitude, generally non-progressive, lacked microscopic correlates, and exhibited evidence of reversibility during recovery. Reversible, mildly increased AST and CK activities were observed in dogs administered 8 mg/kg/day, which likely resulted from vimseltinib mediated inhibition of CSF1R function in macrophages, which has been reported to promote increased AST and CK activities without muscle injury (Radi et al., 2011; Wang et al., 2011).
Urinalysis	No significant treatment-related findings
Organ Weight	No significant treatment-related findings
Microscopic Observations	Microscopic findings included slight to moderate edema in the skin in animals administered 8 mg/kg/day, which correlated with macroscopic observations of swollen head and subcutaneous tissue having a gelatinous appearance. Non-reversible, minimal to moderate mineralization of the epithelium was observed in males administered \ge 4 mg/kg/day. No noteworthy microscopic findings occurred in other tissues, including the liver.
L341: Abbreviations: AUC0-24=area under the concentration versus time curve from 0 to 24 hours; AST=aspartate; BLQ=below the limit of quantitation; CK=creatine kinase; Cmax=maximum drug concentration; CSF1R=colony-stimulating factor 1 receptor; NOAEL=no observed adverse effect level.
L342: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L343: In the 28-day repeat-dose dog toxicology study (Study No. 8332977), vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 2.5, 7.5, 15, or 25 mg/kg/day. Vimseltinib-related moribund euthanasia occurred in all dogs at 15 mg/kg (on Day 24) and 25 mg/kg (on Day 8) during the dosing phase due to poor food consumption, significant body weight loss, and clinical signs of toxicity. Findings in the study included:
L344: - decreases in phosphate, calcium, and APTT
- increases in creatine kinase (up to 207%), AST (up to 159%), ALT (up to 40%), GLDH, fibrinogen, and platelets
L345: Vimseltinib-related microscopic findings included:
L346: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L347: - decreases in zymogen granules and acinar cell degeneration/necrosis in exocrine pancreas
- atrophy, single-cell necrosis, inflammation with infiltrating neutrophils and mucosal hemorrhage in gastrointestinal tract and associated lymphoid tissues (GALT/Peyer's patch)
- infiltrating neutrophils in multiple tissues including liver and heart.
L348: In the 13-week repeat-dose dog toxicology study (Study No. 8403702) with a 4-week recovery phase, vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 2, 4, or 8 mg/kg/day. Findings were similar to the 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study but less severe. Clinical pathology and histopathology data are tabulated in Section 19.3.
L349: In the 39-week repeat-dose dog toxicology study (Study No. 8413879) with a 4-week recovery phase, vimseltinib was administered once daily by oral gavage at 0, 2, 4, or 8 mg/kg/day. Findings were similar to the 13-week repeat-dose toxicology study, and there were fewer microscopic findings in the 39-week study when compared to 4-week dog study with higher doses. In dogs, skin findings included a gelatinous appearance, edema, and immune cell infiltration. Dose-dependent epididymal mineralization in dogs receiving \ge 4 mg/kg/day persisted into recovery.

L359: ## 5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L360: ## The Applicant's Position:
L361: The genotoxic potential for vimseltinib to induce DNA mutations, micronuclei, or unrepaired DNA breaks has been investigated. Vimseltinib tested negative for mutagenicity in a GLP Ames assay, but positive for in vitro micronuclei formation in the absence of metabolic activation when incubated with human lymphocytes for 24 hours at \ge 16 \mu\text{g/mL}. In accordance with ICH S2(R1), the genotoxic potential for vimseltinib was further evaluated in vivo in rats, testing negative for both micronuclei formation in bone marrow and unrepaired DNA breaks in the liver. Based on
L362: ## 4NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 219304}
## ROMVIMZA (vimseltinib)
L363: the results of the in vivo rat micronucleus and alkaline comet study, together with the demonstrated lack of mutagenicity, no additional genetic toxicology studies were conducted. GLP genotoxicity studies conducted with vimseltinib are summarized in Table 11Table 11.

L368: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. Vimseltinib induced micronuclei in cultured human peripheral blood lymphocytes following 24 hours of treatment in the absence of S9.
L369: ## 5.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L370: ## The Applicant's Position:
L371: The Applicant conducted a 26-week carcinogenicity study in 001178-T (hemizygous) RasH2 transgenic mice (DCC-3014-04-0018, eCTD 4.2.3.3.2). Male and female mice were administered vehicle control or 1.0, 4.0, or 12.5 mg/kg/day vimseltinib via oral gavage once daily for up to 26 weeks; or positive control article (MNU) once on Day 1. Dose levels of vimseltinib evaluated in this study were consistent with feedback on the study design provided by the Agency Executive Carcinogenicity Advisory Committee (ECAC). Vimseltinib was well tolerated, and no vimseltinib-related carcinogenic potential was noted for animals administered up to 12.5 mg/kg/day, the highest dose tested. This dose level corresponded to mean C_{max} and AUC_{0-24} values of 7320 ng/mL and 102,000 h*ng/mL, respectively, for vimseltinib and 207 ng/mL and 3380 h*ng/mL, respectively, for DP-7005 (metabolite) on Day 182 of the dosing phase.
L372: - No vimseltinib-related mortality or effects on survival were noted. A clear carcinogenic response was noted for positive control animals. The increased mortality for the positive control group was attributed primarily to the presence of malignant neoplasms.
- No vimseltinib-related food consumption or organ weight effects or macroscopic observations were noted.
- Vimseltinib-related clinical observations for animals administered 12.5 mg/kg/day included a swollen dorsal cervical area and scabs on the dorsal cervical area.
- Vimseltinib-related, increased body weight or body weight gain was noted for males administered \ge 4.0 mg/kg/day and females administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day.
- No vimseltinib-related effects on the occurrence or types of neoplasms were noted. No vimseltinib-related microscopic findings, including mixed cell inflammation in meninges, were noted in the brain of animals at any dose level. Non-neoplastic, vimseltinib-related findings were noted in the spleen, femur, and/or stifle joint. In the spleen, minimally or slightly increased pigmented macrophages were noted in animals administered \ge 1.0 mg/kg/day. In the femur and/or stifle joint, minimal or slight edema was noted in animals administered \ge 4.0 mg/kg/day, with a higher incidence and/or severity in males.
L373: The Applicant is conducting a 104-Week rat carcinogenicity study of vimseltinib in Sprague Dawley rats (DCC-3014-04-0025, eCTD 2.6.7 Table 21).(b) (4)
L374: The Applicant plans to submit the final study report as a postmarketing requirement (PMR).
L375: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L376: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. The 26-week carcinogenicity study in 001178-T (hemizygous) RasH2 transgenic mice (Study DCC-3014-04-0018) was reviewed and presented to the ECAC. ECAC agreed with the Applicant's position.
L377: The Applicant is currently conducting a 104-week carcinogenicity study in Sprague Dawley rats to be submitted as a PMR.

L387: ## Table 13 Embryo-Fetal Development in Rats
L388: Study Number:	DCC-3014-04-0011
Study Title:	An Embryo-Fetal Development Study of DCC-3014 by Oral (Gavage) Administration in Rats
Key Study Findings:	Vimseltinib at a dose of 15 mg/kg/day was a selective developmental toxicant (teratogenic) in rats. Under the conditions of this study at a maternal dose of 15 mg/kg/day malformations of the cardiovascular and skeletal systems occurred. Additional indications of developmental toxicity, including structural anatomic variations and indications of developmental delay, also occurred at this dose.
No adverse maternal effects were attributed to DCC-3014 administration, and therefore, the maternal no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) for DCC-3014 was 15 mg/kg/day DCC-3014: Cmax 19000 ng/mL and AUC(0-t); 313000 hr*ng/mL, the highest dose tested.
Although a limited number of skeletal variations were induced at 2.5 and 5 mg/kg/day, they were attributed to developmental delay that would be anticipated to resolve with continued development and were therefore considered non-adverse. Based on these data, the developmental NOAEL for DCC-3014 was 5 mg/kg/day [DCC-3014: maternal Cmax 2350 ng/mL and AUC(0-t); 40900 hr*ng/mL]. Following administration of DCC-3014, maternal systemic exposure to DCC-3014 and DP-7005 was observed on GD 11, increased with increasing dose for the dose levels evaluated, and was approximately dose-proportional.

GLP compliance:	Yes
